People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… thank you for seeing me. I suppose I came because I haven’t quite been myself since my wife’s fall. I used to be the one who had the lists, the plan, kept things moving. Lately I find myself sitting at the kitchen table just staring at her medication chart, and then I’m… I’m crying, or getting sharp over some tiny thing.

I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but bills are piling up, calls I’m meant to make for mentoring aren’t getting done, even basic housework. It’s like I know what needs doing, but I can’t make myself function. Does that make sense?

Yes… I think I do. I don’t like admitting that, because my wife is the one who actually had the fall, and I feel I ought to just get on with things.

But I feel guilty that I’m not managing properly. I used to be the one with the lists and the plan, and now I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, crying or getting short over nothing. Then I look around and the bills are piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls, the housework isn’t done… and I just think, what’s wrong with me?

So yes, I suppose guilt is a big part of it. Does that make sense?

  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I try not to call it anxiety, because that sounds a bit dramatic, but I do feel on edge.

Since my wife’s fall, I’ve gone from being the man with the lists and the plan to just sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and then I’ll start crying or snap over nothing. And all the things I should be keeping up with — the bills, the mentoring calls, even basic housework — they just pile up, and I feel like I can’t make myself function properly.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F32.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… thank you for seeing me. I suppose I came because I haven’t quite been myself since my wife’s fall. I used to be the one who had the lists, the plan, kept things moving. Lately I find myself sitting at the kitchen table just staring at her medication chart, and then I’m… I’m crying, or getting sharp over some tiny thing.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but bills are piling up, calls I’m meant to make for mentoring aren’t getting done, even basic housework. It’s like I know what needs doing, but I can’t make myself function. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about it in those terms. Things have been so wrapped around looking after her and keeping track of what needs doing that I don’t quite know what I enjoy at the moment.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it—I mean, I can still appreciate a nice cup of tea or a quiet moment, if that’s what you mean. But the usual things… I suppose they just haven’t been much on my mind. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to something clearly and say, “Yes, I no longer enjoy that.” It’s more that I haven’t really made room for those things lately.\n\nI suppose if I tried something I used to like, maybe I’d still enjoy it, but I haven’t quite… tested that, if you see what I mean. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very precise answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I suppose, yes, though I hesitate to say it that strongly. It’s not that I’m sobbing all day, but since my wife’s fall I just don’t feel like myself.\n\nI’ll be sitting at the kitchen table looking at her medication chart and suddenly I’m crying, or I snap over something ridiculous. And then I feel ashamed because bills are piling up, the mentoring calls aren’t getting done, even the housework… I used to be the one with the plan, you know? Now it feels like I can’t quite make myself function. Does that count as sad most of the time?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. I don’t like admitting that, because my wife is the one who actually had the fall, and I feel I ought to just get on with things.\n\nBut I feel guilty that I’m not managing properly. I used to be the one with the lists and the plan, and now I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, crying or getting short over nothing. Then I look around and the bills are piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls, the housework isn’t done… and I just think, what’s wrong with me?\n\nSo yes, I suppose guilt is a big part of it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try not to call it anxiety, because that sounds a bit dramatic, but I do feel on edge.\n\nSince my wife’s fall, I’ve gone from being the man with the lists and the plan to just sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and then I’ll start crying or snap over nothing. And all the things I should be keeping up with — the bills, the mentoring calls, even basic housework — they just pile up, and I feel like I can’t make myself function properly.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s what you mean by anxious, but it does feel like I’m constantly braced for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I wouldn’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a bit different some days, but I couldn’t honestly say I’ve lost a significant amount. Should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s mealtime rather than because I particularly want anything, but I couldn’t honestly say there’s been a clear change in my appetite. I don’t want to overstate it. Is that the sort of thing I should start noticing more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word worthless, exactly… that sounds quite strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI do have moments where I feel I’m not doing very well, or I’m letting things slip, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling worthless. Maybe I’m being a bit careful with the wording. What would you count as that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're a failure since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “I’m a failure,” doctor. That feels… a bit harsh.\n\nI do question myself, yes, and wonder if I’m handling things the right way since her fall. But I don’t know that I often think of myself as a failure exactly. Maybe I’m splitting hairs — I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer it properly?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m just refusing everything.

Yes… I think I have, though I’d like to say it’s not that I don’t care about people.

Does that count as sad most of the time?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. I don’t like admitting that, because my wife is the one who actually had the fall, and I feel I ought to just get on with things.\n\nBut I feel guilty that I’m not managing properly. I used to be the one with the lists and the plan, and now I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, crying or getting short over nothing. Then I look around and the bills are piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls, the housework isn’t done… and I just think, what’s wrong with me?\n\nSo yes, I suppose guilt is a big part of it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try not to call it anxiety, because that sounds a bit dramatic, but I do feel on edge.\n\nSince my wife’s fall, I’ve gone from being the man with the lists and the plan to just sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and then I’ll start crying or snap over nothing. And all the things I should be keeping up with — the bills, the mentoring calls, even basic housework — they just pile up, and I feel like I can’t make myself function properly.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s what you mean by anxious, but it does feel like I’m constantly braced for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I wouldn’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a bit different some days, but I couldn’t honestly say I’ve lost a significant amount. Should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s mealtime rather than because I particularly want anything, but I couldn’t honestly say there’s been a clear change in my appetite. I don’t want to overstate it. Is that the sort of thing I should start noticing more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word worthless, exactly… that sounds quite strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI do have moments where I feel I’m not doing very well, or I’m letting things slip, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling worthless. Maybe I’m being a bit careful with the wording. What would you count as that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're a failure since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “I’m a failure,” doctor. That feels… a bit harsh.\n\nI do question myself, yes, and wonder if I’m handling things the right way since her fall. But I don’t know that I often think of myself as a failure exactly. Maybe I’m splitting hairs — I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m just refusing everything.\n\nI’ve stopped going to some of the mentoring things I used to help with, and a few community commitments as well. I keep telling myself it’s only temporary, but I just… don’t seem to show up. And at home, things I used to manage without even thinking — bills, bits of upkeep, the usual routines — they’ve started slipping quite badly, if I’m honest.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by avoiding activities, but it has changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or meetings with friends and family since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though I’d like to say it’s not that I don’t care about people.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for some of the mentoring and community things I used to do, and I suppose those involved seeing people regularly. I keep meaning to go, or to get back in touch, but then I don’t. And at home, even the ordinary things — bills, upkeep, the basic routines — they’ve been slipping badly.\n\nI’m not proud of that. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding people exactly, but I have definitely pulled back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yes… yes, I would say that was the main thing.

Before her fall, I was the sort of man who had lists and plans for everything. I don’t mean I was perfect, but I kept things moving. Since then, it’s as if I’ve come apart a bit. Should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s mealtime rather than because I particularly want anything, but I couldn’t honestly say there’s been a clear change in my appetite. I don’t want to overstate it. Is that the sort of thing I should start noticing more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word worthless, exactly… that sounds quite strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI do have moments where I feel I’m not doing very well, or I’m letting things slip, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling worthless. Maybe I’m being a bit careful with the wording. What would you count as that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're a failure since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “I’m a failure,” doctor. That feels… a bit harsh.\n\nI do question myself, yes, and wonder if I’m handling things the right way since her fall. But I don’t know that I often think of myself as a failure exactly. Maybe I’m splitting hairs — I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m just refusing everything.\n\nI’ve stopped going to some of the mentoring things I used to help with, and a few community commitments as well. I keep telling myself it’s only temporary, but I just… don’t seem to show up. And at home, things I used to manage without even thinking — bills, bits of upkeep, the usual routines — they’ve started slipping quite badly, if I’m honest.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by avoiding activities, but it has changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or meetings with friends and family since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though I’d like to say it’s not that I don’t care about people.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for some of the mentoring and community things I used to do, and I suppose those involved seeing people regularly. I keep meaning to go, or to get back in touch, but then I don’t. And at home, even the ordinary things — bills, upkeep, the basic routines — they’ve been slipping badly.\n\nI’m not proud of that. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding people exactly, but I have definitely pulled back.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your wife's fall was the main event that triggered these changes in your feelings and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would say that was the main thing.\n\nBefore her fall, I was the sort of man who had lists and plans for everything. I don’t mean I was perfect, but I kept things moving. Since then, it’s as if I’ve come apart a bit. I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart and either end up crying or snapping over something tiny, which I’m not proud of.\n\nAnd things have piled up — bills, housework, calls for the mentoring I used to do. It’s not that I want to let it all go, I just… can’t seem to make myself function the way I did. So yes, I think her fall was what set all this off. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Yes… I have, I suppose. I don’t like admitting that, because some of those things mattered to me.

The mentoring, especially — I used to be quite reliable with that, and there were community commitments I’d go to as well. Since her fall, I’ve just stopped showing up more than I should. I tell myself people understand, or that I’ll get back to it next week, but then I don’t.

And even at home, the things I normally kept on top of without thinking — bills, little repairs, the ordinary household routine — they’re slipping badly. I know that’s not exactly a hobby, but it’s all part of the life I used to manage. Does that answer what you mean?

And all the things I should be keeping up with — the bills, the mentoring calls, even basic housework — they just pile up, and I feel like I can’t make myself function properly.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s what you mean by anxious, but it does feel like I’m constantly braced for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I wouldn’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a bit different some days, but I couldn’t honestly say I’ve lost a significant amount. Should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s mealtime rather than because I particularly want anything, but I couldn’t honestly say there’s been a clear change in my appetite. I don’t want to overstate it. Is that the sort of thing I should start noticing more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word worthless, exactly… that sounds quite strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI do have moments where I feel I’m not doing very well, or I’m letting things slip, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling worthless. Maybe I’m being a bit careful with the wording. What would you count as that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're a failure since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “I’m a failure,” doctor. That feels… a bit harsh.\n\nI do question myself, yes, and wonder if I’m handling things the right way since her fall. But I don’t know that I often think of myself as a failure exactly. Maybe I’m splitting hairs — I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m just refusing everything.\n\nI’ve stopped going to some of the mentoring things I used to help with, and a few community commitments as well. I keep telling myself it’s only temporary, but I just… don’t seem to show up. And at home, things I used to manage without even thinking — bills, bits of upkeep, the usual routines — they’ve started slipping quite badly, if I’m honest.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by avoiding activities, but it has changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or meetings with friends and family since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though I’d like to say it’s not that I don’t care about people.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for some of the mentoring and community things I used to do, and I suppose those involved seeing people regularly. I keep meaning to go, or to get back in touch, but then I don’t. And at home, even the ordinary things — bills, upkeep, the basic routines — they’ve been slipping badly.\n\nI’m not proud of that. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding people exactly, but I have definitely pulled back.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your wife's fall was the main event that triggered these changes in your feelings and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would say that was the main thing.\n\nBefore her fall, I was the sort of man who had lists and plans for everything. I don’t mean I was perfect, but I kept things moving. Since then, it’s as if I’ve come apart a bit. I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart and either end up crying or snapping over something tiny, which I’m not proud of.\n\nAnd things have piled up — bills, housework, calls for the mentoring I used to do. It’s not that I want to let it all go, I just… can’t seem to make myself function the way I did. So yes, I think her fall was what set all this off. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped participating in any hobbies or activities you previously enjoyed with others since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose. I don’t like admitting that, because some of those things mattered to me.\n\nThe mentoring, especially — I used to be quite reliable with that, and there were community commitments I’d go to as well. Since her fall, I’ve just stopped showing up more than I should. I tell myself people understand, or that I’ll get back to it next week, but then I don’t.\n\nAnd even at home, the things I normally kept on top of without thinking — bills, little repairs, the ordinary household routine — they’re slipping badly. I know that’s not exactly a hobby, but it’s all part of the life I used to manage. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very precise answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I suppose, yes, though I hesitate to say it that strongly. It’s not that I’m sobbing all day, but since my wife’s fall I just don’t feel like myself.\n\nI’ll be sitting at the kitchen table looking at her medication chart and suddenly I’m crying, or I snap over something ridiculous. And then I feel ashamed because bills are piling up, the mentoring calls aren’t getting done, even the housework… I used to be the one with the plan, you know? Now it feels like I can’t quite make myself function. Does that count as sad most of the time?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. I don’t like admitting that, because my wife is the one who actually had the fall, and I feel I ought to just get on with things.\n\nBut I feel guilty that I’m not managing properly. I used to be the one with the lists and the plan, and now I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, crying or getting short over nothing. Then I look around and the bills are piling up, I’ve missed mentoring calls, the housework isn’t done… and I just think, what’s wrong with me?\n\nSo yes, I suppose guilt is a big part of it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try not to call it anxiety, because that sounds a bit dramatic, but I do feel on edge.\n\nSince my wife’s fall, I’ve gone from being the man with the lists and the plan to just sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and then I’ll start crying or snap over nothing. And all the things I should be keeping up with — the bills, the mentoring calls, even basic housework — they just pile up, and I feel like I can’t make myself function properly.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s what you mean by anxious, but it does feel like I’m constantly braced for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I wouldn’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a bit different some days, but I couldn’t honestly say I’ve lost a significant amount. Should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s mealtime rather than because I particularly want anything, but I couldn’t honestly say there’s been a clear change in my appetite. I don’t want to overstate it. Is that the sort of thing I should start noticing more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word worthless, exactly… that sounds quite strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI do have moments where I feel I’m not doing very well, or I’m letting things slip, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling worthless. Maybe I’m being a bit careful with the wording. What would you count as that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're a failure since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “I’m a failure,” doctor. That feels… a bit harsh.\n\nI do question myself, yes, and wonder if I’m handling things the right way since her fall. But I don’t know that I often think of myself as a failure exactly. Maybe I’m splitting hairs — I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m just refusing everything.\n\nI’ve stopped going to some of the mentoring things I used to help with, and a few community commitments as well. I keep telling myself it’s only temporary, but I just… don’t seem to show up. And at home, things I used to manage without even thinking — bills, bits of upkeep, the usual routines — they’ve started slipping quite badly, if I’m honest.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by avoiding activities, but it has changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or meetings with friends and family since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though I’d like to say it’s not that I don’t care about people.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for some of the mentoring and community things I used to do, and I suppose those involved seeing people regularly. I keep meaning to go, or to get back in touch, but then I don’t. And at home, even the ordinary things — bills, upkeep, the basic routines — they’ve been slipping badly.\n\nI’m not proud of that. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding people exactly, but I have definitely pulled back.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your wife's fall was the main event that triggered these changes in your feelings and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would say that was the main thing.\n\nBefore her fall, I was the sort of man who had lists and plans for everything. I don’t mean I was perfect, but I kept things moving. Since then, it’s as if I’ve come apart a bit. I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart and either end up crying or snapping over something tiny, which I’m not proud of.\n\nAnd things have piled up — bills, housework, calls for the mentoring I used to do. It’s not that I want to let it all go, I just… can’t seem to make myself function the way I did. So yes, I think her fall was what set all this off. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped participating in any hobbies or activities you previously enjoyed with others since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose. I don’t like admitting that, because some of those things mattered to me.\n\nThe mentoring, especially — I used to be quite reliable with that, and there were community commitments I’d go to as well. Since her fall, I’ve just stopped showing up more than I should. I tell myself people understand, or that I’ll get back to it next week, but then I don’t.\n\nAnd even at home, the things I normally kept on top of without thinking — bills, little repairs, the ordinary household routine — they’re slipping badly. I know that’s not exactly a hobby, but it’s all part of the life I used to manage. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been spending most of your time alone since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, more than I used to, though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’ve cut everyone off completely.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for the mentoring and the community commitments where I’d normally see people. And then at home, I’m not really keeping up the way I should — bills, little house things, the basic routines. They’ve all been slipping more than I’d like to admit.\n\nSo yes, I am spending a lot more time by myself, I think. Does that answer it properly?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s like I know what needs doing, but I can’t make myself function. But the usual things… I suppose they just haven’t been much on my mind. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to find pleasurable but no longer enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to something clearly and say, “Yes, I no longer enjoy that.” It’s more that I haven’t really made room for those things lately.\n\nI suppose if I tried something I used to like, maybe I’d still enjoy it, but I haven’t quite… tested that, if you see what I mean. Now it feels like I can’t quite make myself function. Does that count as sad most of the time?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. I don’t like admitting that, because my wife is the one who actually had the fall, and I feel I ought to just get on with things.\n\nBut I feel guilty that I’m not managing properly. I used to be the one with the lists and the plan, and now I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, crying or getting short over nothing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try not to call it anxiety, because that sounds a bit dramatic, but I do feel on edge.\n\nSince my wife’s fall, I’ve gone from being the man with the lists and the plan to just sitting at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart, and then I’ll start crying or snap over nothing. And all the things I should be keeping up with — the bills, the mentoring calls, even basic housework — they just pile up, and I feel like I can’t make myself function properly.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s what you mean by anxious, but it does feel like I’m constantly braced for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I wouldn’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a bit different some days, but I couldn’t honestly say I’ve lost a significant amount. Should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s mealtime rather than because I particularly want anything, but I couldn’t honestly say there’s been a clear change in my appetite. I don’t want to overstate it. Is that the sort of thing I should start noticing more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word worthless, exactly… that sounds quite strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI do have moments where I feel I’m not doing very well, or I’m letting things slip, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling worthless. Maybe I’m being a bit careful with the wording. What would you count as that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're a failure since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “I’m a failure,” doctor. That feels… a bit harsh.\n\nI do question myself, yes, and wonder if I’m handling things the right way since her fall. But I don’t know that I often think of myself as a failure exactly. Maybe I’m splitting hairs — I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m just refusing everything.\n\nI’ve stopped going to some of the mentoring things I used to help with, and a few community commitments as well. I keep telling myself it’s only temporary, but I just… don’t seem to show up. And at home, things I used to manage without even thinking — bills, bits of upkeep, the usual routines — they’ve started slipping quite badly, if I’m honest.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by avoiding activities, but it has changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or meetings with friends and family since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though I’d like to say it’s not that I don’t care about people.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for some of the mentoring and community things I used to do, and I suppose those involved seeing people regularly. I keep meaning to go, or to get back in touch, but then I don’t. And at home, even the ordinary things — bills, upkeep, the basic routines — they’ve been slipping badly.\n\nI’m not proud of that. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding people exactly, but I have definitely pulled back.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your wife's fall was the main event that triggered these changes in your feelings and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would say that was the main thing.\n\nBefore her fall, I was the sort of man who had lists and plans for everything. I don’t mean I was perfect, but I kept things moving. Since then, it’s as if I’ve come apart a bit. I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart and either end up crying or snapping over something tiny, which I’m not proud of.\n\nAnd things have piled up — bills, housework, calls for the mentoring I used to do. It’s not that I want to let it all go, I just… can’t seem to make myself function the way I did. So yes, I think her fall was what set all this off. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped participating in any hobbies or activities you previously enjoyed with others since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose. I don’t like admitting that, because some of those things mattered to me.\n\nThe mentoring, especially — I used to be quite reliable with that, and there were community commitments I’d go to as well. Since her fall, I’ve just stopped showing up more than I should. I tell myself people understand, or that I’ll get back to it next week, but then I don’t.\n\nAnd even at home, the things I normally kept on top of without thinking — bills, little repairs, the ordinary household routine — they’re slipping badly. I know that’s not exactly a hobby, but it’s all part of the life I used to manage. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been spending most of your time alone since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, more than I used to, though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’ve cut everyone off completely.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for the mentoring and the community commitments where I’d normally see people. And then at home, I’m not really keeping up the way I should — bills, little house things, the basic routines. They’ve all been slipping more than I’d like to admit.\n\nSo yes, I am spending a lot more time by myself, I think. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months now, I think. Since her fall, really — that’s when everything at home changed.\n\nI kept thinking it was just a rough week or two and I’d pull myself together, but it’s carried on longer than I expected. So yes, about two months. Does that sound clear enough?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t feel completely numb all the time, exactly, but I do feel like I’m just moving through the day without really being myself.

Yes… I think I have, though I feel a bit ashamed saying that.

The mentoring used to matter a great deal to me, and the community things too — they gave my week some shape, made me feel useful. Lately I’ve just stopped going, or I let messages sit there and don’t follow through. I tell myself I’ll get back to it when things settle, but I haven’t.

And all the things I should be keeping up with — the bills, the mentoring calls, even basic housework — they just pile up, and I feel like I can’t make myself function properly.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s what you mean by anxious, but it does feel like I’m constantly braced for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I wouldn’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a bit different some days, but I couldn’t honestly say I’ve lost a significant amount. Should I be keeping track of that?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I haven’t been paying close attention to it the way I probably should.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s mealtime rather than because I particularly want anything, but I couldn’t honestly say there’s been a clear change in my appetite. I don’t want to overstate it. Is that the sort of thing I should start noticing more carefully?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word worthless, exactly… that sounds quite strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI do have moments where I feel I’m not doing very well, or I’m letting things slip, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling worthless. Maybe I’m being a bit careful with the wording. That feels… a bit harsh.\n\nI do question myself, yes, and wonder if I’m handling things the right way since her fall. But I don’t know that I often think of myself as a failure exactly. Maybe I’m splitting hairs — I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m just refusing everything.\n\nI’ve stopped going to some of the mentoring things I used to help with, and a few community commitments as well. I keep telling myself it’s only temporary, but I just… don’t seem to show up. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, doctor. I haven’t really been keeping track, to be honest.

My clothes don’t seem dramatically different or anything like that, so I wouldn’t want to say I’ve lost a noticeable amount if I’m not certain. I can weigh myself if that would be useful.

And all the things I should be keeping up with — the bills, the mentoring calls, even basic housework — they just pile up, and I feel like I can’t make myself function properly.\n\nI’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s what you mean by anxious, but it does feel like I’m constantly braced for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I wouldn’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a bit different some days, but I couldn’t honestly say I’ve lost a significant amount. Maybe I’m being a bit careful with the wording. That feels… a bit harsh.\n\nI do question myself, yes, and wonder if I’m handling things the right way since her fall. But I don’t know that I often think of myself as a failure exactly. Maybe I’m splitting hairs — I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m just refusing everything.\n\nI’ve stopped going to some of the mentoring things I used to help with, and a few community commitments as well. I keep telling myself it’s only temporary, but I just… don’t seem to show up. And at home, things I used to manage without even thinking — bills, bits of upkeep, the usual routines — they’ve started slipping quite badly, if I’m honest.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by avoiding activities, but it has changed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or meetings with friends and family since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, though I’d like to say it’s not that I don’t care about people.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for some of the mentoring and community things I used to do, and I suppose those involved seeing people regularly. I keep meaning to go, or to get back in touch, but then I don’t. And at home, even the ordinary things — bills, upkeep, the basic routines — they’ve been slipping badly.\n\nI’m not proud of that. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding people exactly, but I have definitely pulled back.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your wife's fall was the main event that triggered these changes in your feelings and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would say that was the main thing.\n\nBefore her fall, I was the sort of man who had lists and plans for everything. I don’t mean I was perfect, but I kept things moving. Since then, it’s as if I’ve come apart a bit. I’ll sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart and either end up crying or snapping over something tiny, which I’m not proud of.\n\nAnd things have piled up — bills, housework, calls for the mentoring I used to do. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped participating in any hobbies or activities you previously enjoyed with others since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose. I don’t like admitting that, because some of those things mattered to me.\n\nThe mentoring, especially — I used to be quite reliable with that, and there were community commitments I’d go to as well. Since her fall, I’ve just stopped showing up more than I should. I tell myself people understand, or that I’ll get back to it next week, but then I don’t.\n\nAnd even at home, the things I normally kept on top of without thinking — bills, little repairs, the ordinary household routine — they’re slipping badly. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been spending most of your time alone since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, more than I used to, though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’ve cut everyone off completely.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for the mentoring and the community commitments where I’d normally see people. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your wife's fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months now, I think.
